Title: The Doctrines and Discipline of the Methodist Episcopal Church in America, with explanatory notes by Thomas Coke and Francis Asbury; the tenth edition
Creator: The Methodist Episcopal Church; notes by Thomas Coke (1747-1814) and Francis Asbury (1745-1816)
Publisher: Philadelphia: printed by Henry Tuckniss, sold by John Dickins, No. 41 Market Street
Publication Date: 1798
Rights: Public Domain (published 1798, more than 95 years ago; public domain by date under the project's rolling rule; the item's own rights statement reads 'NOT_IN_COPYRIGHT')
Source: Internet Archive item doctrinesanddis18churgoog (https://archive.org/details/doctrinesanddis18churgoog), the item's own doctrinesanddis18churgoog_djvu.txt full-text OCR derivative; contributing library per the item metadata: Oxford University; host metadata date: 1798. Provenance note: Header prepended by the Library thread; the host's front matter up to and including the line beginning 'at |http: //books' removed (47 lines); nothing else altered, no OCR error corrected, nothing retyped. Google Books scan from the Bodleian; the Google notice at the head of the file is removed as recorded above; the title page is partly garbled, the text readable with the long s kept.
Content note: The American church's Discipline of 1798 with the bishops' own annotations: the Articles of Religion, the General Rules, the government of conferences, elders, deacons and class meetings, each section glossed by Coke and Asbury; the Twenty-Five Articles in the church's own book.

NOTES. 

Our foclety may be confidcred as a fpiritual hofpital, where 
fouls come to be cured of their fpiritual difeafes. The members 
therefore who compofe our clafs meetings vary exceedingly in the 
flate of their minds and the degrees of their experience. On this ac- 
count it was thought necelTary by our venerable leader Mr. 
Wefley, to eftablifh a fociety of evangelical believers within the , 
fociety compofed of the whole body of Methodifts, to which he 
gave the name of the band-foaety. This inflitution he borrowed, 
from the pratftice of the primitive churches, as indeed he did al- 
moft every thing he edabliihed. 

The heart of man by nature is fuch a cage of unclean birds, 
that few are to be found who will lay before their brethren all. 
its fccret movements, uiilefs the love of God be the ruling princi- 
ple of their fouls. And even then they are not called upon to- 
exercife this confidence, except towai ds a fmall confidential comi 
pany of true believers like themfelves. When bands can be form-
